as well, resulting in a contradiction. We argue the reverse direction as well i.e., let
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}\supset\underset{\text{all possible orientations of }G}{\cup}\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Then, there exists a tuple
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}
such that
\mathbf{t}\not\in\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
for every orientation of
G
. Note that this implies for each relation
R_{({v},{u})}
, we have
\pi_{({v},{u})}(\mathbf{t})\in R_{({v},{u})}
, which in turn implies
\pi_{({v},{u})}(\mathbf{t})=R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
(or)
\pi_{({v},{u})}(\mathbf{t})=R_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}
. As a result,
\mathbf{t}
will be present in the join out of some orientation of
J
, resulting in a contradiction. This proves (
20
).

To complete the proof, we argue (
19
), which follows from the proof above as well i.e., for any orientation of
G
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}

\displaystyle\supseteq\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},

which in turns implies (
19
) for the orientation that achieves the maximum in (
18
).
∎

A.4.2
Computing the Optimal Orientation

Give an undirected graph
H=(V,E)
, let
\sigma
denote an orientation of the edge set
E
s.t. for every
\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in E
, we have
\sigma(\left\{{u},{v}\right\})\in\left\{({u}\rightarrow{v}),({v}\rightarrow{u})\right\}
, i.e. we pick exactly one of the two possible orientations for the undirected edge. Given an orientation
\sigma
, define the corresponding ‘degree vector’
\mathbf{d}_{\sigma}
such that
\mathbf{d}_{\sigma}(u)
is the
outdegree
of
u\in V
under this orientation.
We consider the following problem:

Problem A.8
.

Given
p\in[1,\infty)
and
H=(V,E)
, compute a
\sigma
such that
\left\lVert\mathbf{d}_{\sigma}\right\rVert_{p}
is minimized.

Note that the problem for
p=1
is trivial (since all orientations give the same norm value of
|E|
) while the problem for
p=\infty
is the problem of computing degeneracy of
H
(which has a well-known linear time algorithm).

In this appendix, we show that the above problem can be solved in polynomial time for any fixed
p\in[1,\infty)
.
The results in this section are due to Shi Li. We thank him for allowing us to use his proof.

The high level idea is to come up with a convex programming relaxation of the above problem and then we argue that there indeed exists an integral solution (and that one can round a fractional optimal solution into an optimal integral one as well).

Before we state the convex program, we define a piece-wise linear function that agrees with the
\ell_{p}
norm for all integer values. In particular, define:

f_{p}(x)=\left({\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor}\right)^{p}\cdot\left(1-x+{\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor}\right)+\left({\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor}+1\right)^{p}\cdot\left(x-{\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor}\right).

Indeed, note that if
x
is an integer, we have
f(x)=x^{p}
, as desired. Also note that the function is convex (which in turn follows from the facts that
x^{p}
is convex for
p\geq 1
and that
f_{p}(x)
is piece-wise linear in between two integral values).

Now consider the following convex program:

\displaystyle\min\sum_{u\in V}f_{p}\left(d_{u}\right)

(21)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}+x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=1\text{ for every }\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in E

(22)

\displaystyle d_{u}=\sum_{\left\{{u},{w}\right\}\in E}x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}\text{ for every }u\in V

(23)

\displaystyle x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})},x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\geq 0\text{ for every}\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in E

Note that any integral solution must have
x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})},x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\in\left\{0,1\right\}
and corresponds to an orientation
\sigma
(
x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}=1
implies that
\sigma\left(\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\right)=({u}\rightarrow{v})
and
x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}=0
implies that
\sigma\left(\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\right)=({v}\rightarrow{u})
) and
d_{u}
then corresponds to the outdegree of
u
under
\sigma
. Further, by our earlier observation on
f_{p}(\cdot)
, the objective function corresponds to the objective function of our problem. Thus, if we can compute the optimal integral solution to the above convex program, then we will be done.

First, we recall the well known result that the convex program above can be solved in polynomial time (since the objective is convex and all constraints are linear). However, such a solution is not guaranteed to be integral. We argue next that we can convert an optimal fractional solution into an optimal integral solution in polynomial time (and hence also argue that the convex program also always has an optimal integral solution).

Theorem A.9
.

Let
\mathbf{x}=\left(x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})},x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in E}
be an optimal solution to the convex program (
21
). Then in polynomial time, we can convert this into an integral optimal solution.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{x}
is integral then we have nothing to argue so we assume there is at least one
\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in E
such that
x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})},x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\in(0,1)
– for notational simplicity we will state that the undirected edge
\left\{{u},{v}\right\}
is
fractional
. Let
d_{u}
for every
u\in V
be defined by (
23
).

We make the following simple observation that will be useful later on. Let
u\in V
and let
d_{u}
be integral. Then if there is a fractional edge
\left\{{u},{w}\right\}
, then there has to be at least one more fractional edge
\left\{{u},{v}\right\}
for
w\neq v
. (This follows because if exactly one edge incident to
u
is fractional, then by (
23
),
d_{u}
cannot be an integer.)

In the rest of the proof, we present a polynomial time procedure
27
27

27

We will not explicitly argue the runtime of the procedure below but its description immediately implies the claimed polynomial runtime.
that creates a new optimal solution
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to (
21
) such that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
has strictly fewer number of fractional edges or fractional degree values
d_{u}
(than those for
\mathbf{x}
). Note that this is enough to prove our claimed result (since the procedure below can be run at most
|V|+|E|
to get the desired integral optimal solution).

We first consider the case when all
d_{u}
values are integers. Let
\left\{{w},{v}\right\}
be any fractional edge. Then by the observation above there exists an fractional edge
\left\{{v},{y}\right\}
for
y\neq w
. Thus, we can ‘move‘ from
w
to
y
. We can continue this process by picking a fractional edge to get a new node till we end up with a cycle
C
(and such a cycle has to exists since there are
|V|
vertices). Now define,

\epsilon=\min_{\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in C}\min\left\{x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})},x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\}.

(24)

Note that by construction of
C
,
\epsilon>0
. We will construct two related feasible solutions from
\mathbf{x}
that both are also optimal and at least one of them now has at least one less fractional edge. Let the vertices in
C
in order be
u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{c-1},u_{0}
. Then we define two solution
\mathbf{x}^{+}
and
\mathbf{x}^{-}
as follows. Both these solutions agree with
\mathbf{x}
for all edges not in
C
. Otherwise for every
0\leq i<c
, we have

\displaystyle x^{+}_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}})}

\displaystyle=x_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}})}+\epsilon

\displaystyle x^{+}_{({u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}

\displaystyle=x_{({u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}-\epsilon

\displaystyle x^{-}_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}})}

\displaystyle=x_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}})}-\epsilon

\displaystyle x^{-}_{({u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}

\displaystyle=x_{({u_{(i+1)\mod{c}}}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}+\epsilon

i.e. in
\mathbf{x}^{+}
the ‘clockwise’ directions get increased by
\epsilon
and the ‘counter-clockwise’ directions get decreased by
\epsilon
(and this gets flipped in
\mathbf{x}^{-}
). Let
d^{+}_{u}
and
d^{-}_{u}
be the corresponding degree values defined by (
23
). It can be verified that both
\mathbf{x}^{+}
and
\mathbf{x}^{-}
are still feasible solutions and
d^{+}_{u}=d^{-}_{u}=d_{u}
(and hence, both
\mathbf{x}^{+}
and
\mathbf{x}^{-}
are still optimal). Finally, note that by definition of
\epsilon
in (
24
), in either
\mathbf{x}^{+}
or
\mathbf{x}^{-}
at least one edge in
C
is no longer fractional.

Finally, we consider the case when not all
d_{u}
are integers. Since we have that
\sum_{u\in V}d_{u}=|E|
(which in turn follows by summing up (
22
) over all
\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in E
and then noting each outgoing edge appears exactly once in this sum), which is integer. Hence, this implies there has to be at least two vertices with non-integral
d
values. Let
s\in V
such that
d_{s}
is not an integer. This implies that there exists an incident edge
\left\{{s},{u}\right\}
that is not fractional (because if not,
d_{s}
will be an integer). Now we keep on adding incident fractional edges as we did in the first case. Here we stop in case we get a cycle with all vertices
u
in the cycle have an integer
d_{u}
(in which case we just run the argument from the first case above) else we end up with another
t\neq s
such that
d_{t}
is not an integer. In other words, we end up with an
s-t
path
\mathcal{P}
. Now define

\epsilon=\min\left\{d_{s}-{\left\lfloor d_{s}\right\rfloor},d_{t}-{\left\lfloor d_{t}\right\rfloor},{\left\lceil d_{s}\right\rceil}-d_{s},{\left\lceil d_{t}\right\rceil}-d_{t},\min_{\left\{{u},{v}\right\}\in\mathcal{P}}\min\left\{x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})},x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\}\right\}.

(25)

Construct
\mathbf{x}^{+}
by increasing the
x_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}
values (by
\epsilon
) that are in the directed
s\to t
path and decreasing the
x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
values (by
\epsilon
) that are in the directed
t\to s
path. Similarly we get
\mathbf{x}^{-}
by replacing
\epsilon
by
-\epsilon
in the definition of
\mathbf{x}^{+}
. (
\mathbf{x}^{+},\mathbf{x}^{-}
and
\mathbf{x}
are exactly the same outside of edges in
\mathcal{P}
.) Let
d^{+}_{u}
and
d-_{u}
be the corresponding degree values defined by (
23
). It can be again verified that both
\mathbf{x}^{+}
and
\mathbf{x}^{-}
are still feasible solutions. Further it can be verified that for all vertices
u
in
\mathcal{P}
other than
s
and
t
we have
d^{+}_{u}=d^{-}_{u}=d_{u}
. The only degree values that can change are those of
s
and
t
(and indeed we have
d^{+}_{s}=d_{s}+\epsilon,d^{+}_{t}=d_{t}-\epsilon,d^{-}_{s}=d_{s}-\epsilon,d^{-}_{t}=d_{t}+\epsilon
). Finally, by the choice of
\epsilon
either in
\mathbf{x}^{+}
or
\mathbf{x}^{-}
at least one edge is no more fractional
or
at least one vertex will no longer have a fractional degree value.

To complete the proof we argue that either
f_{p}(d^{+}_{s})+f_{p}(d^{+}_{t})-f_{p}(d_{s})-f_{p}(d_{t})
or
f_{p}(d^{-}_{s})+f_{p}(d^{-}_{t})-f_{p}(d_{s})-f_{p}(d_{t})
is at most zero (which note will complete the proof). To argue this, we inspect these differences. For notational convenience define
D_{s}={\left\lfloor d_{s}\right\rfloor}
and
D_{t}={\left\lfloor d_{t}\right\rfloor}
. Then by definition of
\epsilon
in (
25
), we have
{\left\lfloor d^{+}_{s}\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor d^{-}_{s}\right\rfloor}=D_{s}
and
{\left\lfloor d^{+}_{t}\right\rfloor}={\left\lfloor d^{-}_{t}\right\rfloor}=D_{t}
. Further, note that by definition of
f_{p}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle f_{p}(d^{+}_{s})

\displaystyle=f_{p}(d_{s}+\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}\cdot(1-d_{s}-\epsilon+D_{s})+\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}\cdot(d_{s}+\epsilon-D_{s})

\displaystyle=\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}\cdot(1-d_{s}+D_{s})+\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}\cdot(d_{s}-D_{s})+\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}\right)

\displaystyle=f_{p}(d_{s})+\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}\right).

Similarly, we get

\displaystyle f_{p}(d^{-}_{s})

\displaystyle=f_{p}(d_{s})-\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}\right)

\displaystyle f_{p}(d^{+}_{t})

\displaystyle=f_{p}(d_{t})+\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{t}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{t}\right)^{p}\right)

\displaystyle f_{p}(d^{-}_{t})

\displaystyle=f_{p}(d_{t})-\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{t}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{t}\right)^{p}\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle f_{p}(d^{+}_{s})-f_{p}(d_{s})+f_{p}(d^{+}_{t})-f_{p}(d_{t})=\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}+\left(1+D_{t}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{t}\right)^{p}\right)

\displaystyle f_{p}(d^{-}_{s})-f_{p}(d_{s})+f_{p}(d^{-}_{t})-f_{p}(d_{t})=-\epsilon\cdot\left(\left(1+D_{s}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{s}\right)^{p}+\left(1+D_{t}\right)^{p}-\left(D_{t}\right)^{p}\right).

From the above is it easy to see that at least one of
f_{p}(d^{+}_{s})+f_{p}(d^{+}_{t})-f_{p}(d_{s})-f_{p}(d_{t})
or
f_{p}(d^{-}_{s})+f_{p}(d^{-}_{t})-f_{p}(d_{s})-f_{p}(d_{t})
is at most zero, as desired.
∎

Appendix B
Missing Details in Section
2

B.1
\mathsf{PANDA}
as a Substitute for Algorithm
2.2

In this section, our goal is to show that
\mathsf{PANDA}
be used as a subsitute for Algorithm
2.2
. We start by noting that
\mathsf{PANDA}

[
17
]
is traditionally designed for
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{\infty}
norms. We note here that with a simple modification, it can be used in place of our Algorithm
2.2
for general
\ell_{p}
-norm constraints as well, reflecting
\mathsf{PANDA}
’s generality. To see this, recall our runtime upper bound
\mathcal{D}_{u}(\mathbf{d})
from Theorem
4.1
:

\displaystyle\min\left\{\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\min}\left\{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\},\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\min}\left\{\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}\right\}\right\}.

(26)

While the terms
d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
can be modelled as standard
\ell_{\infty}
bounds, the terms of the form

\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}
can be equivalently represented as a bound on the domain size
|\mathrm{Dom}(u)|
of
u
(which can in turn be modeled as an
\ell_{\infty}
bound on a relation
({\emptyset}\rightarrow{u})
). As a result, in addition to
d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
,
\mathsf{PANDA}
needs to know the values
L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}
for every
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
to construct a proof sequence in this case whereas we do not this knowledge in our Algorithm
2.2
. Assuming that using
\mathsf{PANDA}
for each degree configuration
\mathbf{d}
achieves the polymatroid bound in this setup, note here that we would still end up losing a multiplicative factor of
O\left(\log{N}^{\left(\left(2^{|V|}\right)!\right)^{2}}\right)
in its runtime, which we avoid in our algorithm. Using
\mathsf{PANDA}
removes the need for Algorithm
2.2
and Algorithm
2.2
. Further, note that in Algorithm
2.2
, we compute
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}
as the union of all
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})
s and as a result, by using
\mathsf{PANDA}
for each
\mathbf{d}
, we would lose another factor of
\log^{|E|}L
(by upper bounding the algorithm’s runtime with the
\mathbf{d}
achieving the maximum upper bound). However, we can ignore it since it is smaller than
O\left(\log{N}^{\left(\left(2^{|V|}\right)!\right)^{2}}\right)
.

To complete this discussion of using
\mathsf{PANDA}
in our setup, we still need to show that the hard instance (with
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{\infty}
bounds) for each
\mathbf{d}
(corresponding to the polymatroid bound) satisfies the
\ell_{p}
-norm bound. It turns out that these hard instances
[
17
]
always have uniform
\ell_{\infty}
bounds i.e., for relations
R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
and
R_{({\infty}\rightarrow{u})}
for every
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
, the corresponding
\ell_{\infty}
bounds are
\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\min}d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
and
\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\min}\left(\frac{L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}\right)
(note that here we use
L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
in place of
L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}
and this is ok to do since the latter is at most the former). Note that this implies the
\ell_{p}
-norm of each sub-relation
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
in the instance is upper bounded by
\sqrt[p]{\frac{L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
, which is at most
L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Since this instance is valid for any degree configuration
\mathbf{d}
, it holds for the maximum among
\mathbf{d}
s as well.

B.2
Question
1.4
for General
\ell_{p}
-norm Constraints

In this section, we revisit Question
1.4
for General
\ell_{p}
-norm constraints, which we considered earlier in Section
2.4
. We present an example below to demonstrate the answer to Question
1.4
is
no
.

Example B.1
.

Consider the triangle query
R({A}\rightarrow{B})\bowtie S({B}\rightarrow{C})\bowtie T({C}\rightarrow{A})

where each relation has an
\ell_{2}
bound of
L
. Now consider any acyclic-subgraph, e.g., the subquery
R({A}\rightarrow{B})\bowtie S({B}\rightarrow{C})
. It is easy to see that in the worst-case this subquery can have a join output size of at least
L^{5/2}
– for e.g.,

\displaystyle R({A}\rightarrow{B})=[L^{2}]\times[1]\text{ and }S({B}\rightarrow{C})=[1]\times[\sqrt{L}].

It is easy to verify that both relation instances have an
\ell_{2}
bound of
L
and the join output size for the above instance for
R\bowtie S
is
L^{5/2}
. On the other hand, in Example
2.2
we have already shown that for the triangle query, the tight bound on the join query size is
\Theta(L^{2})
, while this example shows that the best acyclic subgraph join output size bound is
\Omega\left(L^{5/2}\right)
.

\blacktriangleleft

B.3
Acyclic Triangle Upper Bound using Cauchy-Schwartz

In this section, we present an upper bound argument for the case when
G
is (what we call) an acyclic triangle (i.e., it is cyclic in the undirected sense but acyclic in the directed sense).

Example B.2
.

G
has
V=\{A,B,C\}
and
E=\{({A}\rightarrow{B}),({B}\rightarrow{C}),({A}\rightarrow{C})\}
with relations
R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})},S_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}
and
T_{({A}\rightarrow{C})}
. Further, we assume the
\ell_{2}
-norm case and
\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\max}\left\lVert R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\rVert_{2}\leq L
.

Our goal is to prove an upper bound of
L^{2}
on
R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}\bowtie S_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}\bowtie T_{({A}\rightarrow{C})}
. For any
a\in\mathrm{Dom}(A)
, let
\deg_{R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}}(a)
and
\deg_{T_{({A}\rightarrow{C})}}(a)
denote the outdegree of
a
in
R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}
and
T_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}
. Then note that the output size of the join is:

\displaystyle\underset{a\in\mathrm{Dom}(A)}{\sum}\deg_{R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}}(a)\cdot\deg_{T_{({A}\rightarrow{C})}}(a)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\underset{a\in\mathrm{Dom}(A)}{\sum}\deg_{R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}}(a)^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\underset{a\in\mathrm{Dom}(A)}{\sum}\deg_{T_{({A}\rightarrow{C})}}(a)^{2}}

\displaystyle=L\cdot L=L^{2},

where the inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwartz.

\blacktriangleleft

Appendix C
Missing Details in Section
3

The standard results below will be used in our analysis.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
n
be a positive integer,
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
be non-negative real numbers and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\geq 0
such that
\underset{i\in[n]}{\sum}x_{i}\geq 1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}a_{i}\leq\underset{i\in[n]}{\prod}a_{i}^{x_{i}}.

Lemma C.2

(Hölder’s inequality
[
11
]
)
.

Let
m,n
be positive integers and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\geq 0
such that
\underset{i\in[n]}{\sum}x_{i}\geq 1
. Let
a_{ij}\geq 0
be non-negative real numbers for
i\in[m]
and
j\in[n]
. We have

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[m]}{\sum}\quad\underset{j\in[n]}{\prod}a_{ij}^{x_{i}}\leq\underset{j\in[n]}{\prod}\left(\underset{i\in[m]}{\sum}a_{ij}\right)^{x_{i}},

assuming the convention
0^{0}=0
.

C.1
Effective Domain Size Upper Bound based on Outdegree

For a fixed degree configuration
\mathbf{d}=(d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
, let
\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(v)
denote the effective domain size of
v
on
R^{d_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}}_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}
for every
({u}\rightarrow{v})\in E
and
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{w})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{w})}
for every
({v}\rightarrow{w})\in E
, for every
v\in V
.

Lemma C.3
.

Fix
p:1\leq p\leq\infty
. We have for every
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(v)\right|\leq\left(\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}^{p}}{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}\right).

Proof.

We start by recalling that the subrelation
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
satisifes the degree constraint
d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
i.e., each value of
v\in\mathrm{Dom}(v)
has degree at least
\frac{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}{2}+1
and at most
d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
.

The proof is by contradiction. We assume

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(v)\right|>\left(\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}^{p}}{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}\right).

(27)

Since each tuple in
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
satisfies (
1
), we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right\rVert^{p}_{p}

\displaystyle\geq\left|\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(v)\right|\cdot\underset{x\in\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(u)}{\min}\left|D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x)\right|^{p}

\displaystyle>\left|\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(v)\right|\cdot\frac{d^{p}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}{2^{p}}

(28)

\displaystyle>\left(\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}^{p}}{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}\right)\cdot\frac{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}{2^{p}}

(29)

\displaystyle=L^{p}_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}.

Here, (
28
) follows from the fact stated above i.e., each value in
\mathrm{Dom}(v)
has degree at least
d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
in
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Then, (
29
) follows from (
27
). Note that this contradicts
\left\lVert R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right\rVert_{p}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}
and as a result, we have shown that
\left|\mathrm{Dom}_{\mathbf{d}}(v)\right|\leq\left(\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}^{p}}{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{p}}\right)
, completing the proof.
∎

C.2
More Details on Data Structures for our Algorithms

In this section, we talk a bit more about our B-tree data structure. Recall that we store each relation
R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
as a B-tree-like index structure
[
4
]
. For this B-tree, the first level is indexed by
(v)
(i.e., all values in
\pi_{v}(R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})
) and the second level is indexed by
(u,\mathrm{val}_{v})
, where
\mathrm{val}_{v}\in\pi_{v}(R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})
. Note that this B-tree can be constructed in time
O(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\log(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}))
. Since we construct such a B-tree for each
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
, the total preprocessing time is
O(|E|\cdot L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\log(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}))
. Note that this time will be superseded by the final runtime of our join algorithm.

C.3
Decomposing
R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
into buckets based on degree

In this section, we state and prove a standard result on decomposing each relation
R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
into a logarithmic number of buckets.

Lemma C.4
.

Given any relation
R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
, it can be decomposed into a union of
W
subrelations (some of which can be empty), where each subrelation satisfies a degree constraint
d^{w}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=2^{w}
for some
w\in[W]
. Further, we have
W\leq\log(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})+1
.

Proof.

We start by defining

\displaystyle D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x)=\left\{(x,y)\in R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}:y\in\mathrm{Dom}(u)\right\}.

(30)

Next, we define for every
w\in[W]
:

\displaystyle R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=\underset{x\in\mathrm{Dom}(v):2^{w-1}\leq|D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x)|\leq 2^{w}}{\cup}D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x),

(31)

where
D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x)
is defined above. Note that each
R^{w}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
satisfies the degree constraint
d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=2^{w}
. Further, we have
\underset{w\in[W]}{\cup}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
since for every
x\in\mathrm{Dom}(v)
,
D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x)\in R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
, for some
w\in[W]
.

Since
\left\lVert R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\rVert_{p}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
, we have

\displaystyle 2^{W-1}

\displaystyle\leq\underset{x\in\mathrm{Dom}(v)}{\max}\left|D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=2^{\log(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})},

where the final inequality follows by the fact that
\left\lVert D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\rVert_{p}\geq\left\lVert D_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right\rVert_{\infty}
. This implies
W\leq\log\left(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)+1
.
∎

C.4
Computing
|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}|
as the union of
|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})|
s

Recall that our algorithm (Algorithm
2.2
) computes the final join output as the union of
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})
across all degree configurations
\mathbf{d}
. We prove the following standard result.

\displaystyle|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}|\leq\underset{\mathbf{d}=(d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}}{\sum}|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})|.

(32)

We argue the following result, which immediately implies the above.

\displaystyle\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}=\underset{\mathbf{d}=(d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}}{\cup}\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d}),

(33)

where recall that

\displaystyle\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})=\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}.

We start by claiming that based on our decomposition above, the following is true for every
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
:

\displaystyle\underset{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\leq L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}{\cup}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}.

In particular, this follows from the fact that each
x\in\mathrm{Dom}(v):(x,\cdot)\in R_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
belongs to an unique
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
(by definition of
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
). Note that this implies for every output tuple
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}
, we have
\pi_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}(\mathbf{t})
(which is
\mathbf{t}
projected down to attributes
v
and
u
) belongs to a unique
R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
and gives us (
33
), as required.

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We start by recalling
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G
:

\displaystyle\min\quad\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\log(L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})

(
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
)

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\sum}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}\geq 1\quad\forall u\in V

(34)

\displaystyle x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\geq 0\quad\forall({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E.

(35)

We also restate Theorem
3.2
here before proving it.

Theorem D.1
.

For any directed graph
G=(V,E)
, there exists an optimal solution
\mathbf{x}^{*}=\left(x^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
and a
t
such that
G
can be decomposed into a disjoint union of
t
connected components (in the undirected sense)
G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i})
with

\displaystyle|V_{i}|-1\leq|E(G_{i})|\leq|V_{i}|,\text{ where }x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0\quad\forall({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})

and
\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}=\left(x^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}
is an optimal basic feasible solution (see Definition
D.2
) for
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. Further, we have
\cup_{i=1}^{t}V(G_{i})=V
and
V(G_{i})\cap V(G_{j})=\emptyset

\forall i,j\in[t],i\neq j
. The following is true:

\displaystyle\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}

\displaystyle=\times_{i\in[t]}\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(G_{i}).

We define some standard preliminaries on linear programs needed to prove this result.

D.1
Preliminaries and Existing Results

We start by recalling the definition of a basic feasible solution, specialized to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
.

Definition D.2

(Basic Feasible Solution to LP (
3
)
[
25
]
)
.

A basic feasible solution
\mathbf{x}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
is one that satisfies all its
|V|+|E|
constraints with at least
|E|
of them satisfied with equality (ones we call tight). Let
C
denote the
(|V|+|E|)\times|E|
constraint matrix, where the rows are indexed by constraints and columns are indexed by variables and let
S
denote the set of tight constraints. Then, the matrix
C
projected down to rows (i.e., constraints) in
S
has rank exactly
|E|
.

We will invoke the following well-known theorem in our arguments.

Theorem D.3

(From
[
25
]
)
.

There always exists an optimal solution to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
that is basic feasible.

D.2
Main Proof

To prove Theorem
3.2
, we will use the following lemma.

Lemma D.4
.

For any directed graph
G=(V,E)
and for every basic feasible solution
\mathbf{x}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
there exists a
t
such that
G
can be decomposed into a disjoint union of
t
connected components (in the undirected sense)
G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i})
such that
|V_{i}|-1\leq|E_{i}|\leq|V_{i}|
and
\mathbf{x}_{i}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E_{i}}
is a basic feasible solution for LP (
3
) on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. Further, we have
\cup_{i=1}^{t}V(G_{i})=V
and
V(G_{i})\cap V(G_{j})=\emptyset

\forall i,j\in[t],i\neq j
.

Assuming the above lemma is true, we prove Theorem
3.2
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Invoking Theorem
D.3
, there exists an optimal solution
\mathbf{x}^{*}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
that is basic feasible. Now, we can use Lemma
D.4
on
G
to decompose
\mathbf{x}^{*}
into
t
connected components (for some
t>0
) such that
G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i})
such that
|V_{i}|-1\leq|E_{i}|\leq|V_{i}|
and
\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E_{i}}
is a basic feasible solution for LP (
3
) on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. Our goal here is to argue that the solution
\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}
(defined above) is optimal for every
i\in[t]
.

The proof is by contradiction. In particular, we show that if
\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i}
is not optimal for some
i\in[t]
, then we would contradict the fact that
\mathbf{x}
is optimal. Assume otherwise i.e., there exists an alternative optimal basic feasible solution
28
28

28

Note that there always exists such a solution by Theorem
D.3

\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
such that

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}

\displaystyle<\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}.

(36)

We define
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=\mathbf{x}^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
for every
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E\setminus E(G_{i})
. The new solution
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is (basic) feasible by our assumption for
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
and by construction for the remaining edges. Computing the objective value of
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}

\displaystyle=\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E\setminus E(G_{i})}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right)\cdot\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}

\displaystyle<\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E\setminus E(G_{i})}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right)\cdot\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}{\prod}L_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}},

where the inequality follows from (
36
). Since
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
has a smaller objective value than
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, this contradicts the optimality of
\mathbf{x}^{*}
and completes the proof.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
3.2
, we prove Lemma
D.4
.

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

We start by recalling the constraints of
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G
:

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\sum}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}\geq 1\qquad\forall u\in V

\displaystyle x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\geq 0\qquad\forall({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E.

(37)

Then, the
(|V|+|E|)\times|E|
constraint matrix
C
is defined as follows (in the order of the constraints). The first
|V|
rows are indexed by vertices in
V
and the next
|E|
rows are indexed by variables in
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
. The columns are indexed by variables of LP i.e.,
(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
. For every
u\in V
, note that
C\left[u,x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right]=1
and
C\left[u,x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}\right]=\frac{1}{p}
, where
v,w\in V
and
({v}\rightarrow{u}),({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E
. For every edge
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
, note that
C\left[({v}\rightarrow{u}),x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right]=1
. All the remaining entries in
C
are
0
.

Consider any basic feasible solution
\mathbf{x}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
. We remove every edge
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
from
G
that satisfies
x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
. Note that this process does not remove any vertex from
G
since

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\sum}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}\geq 1

for every
u\in V(G)
(by definition of
\mathbf{x}
).

Let the resulting graph after this process be
G_{1}
and for simplicity, we assume
G_{1}
is a single connected component in the undirected sense (else we consider the components individually), which implies

\displaystyle|E(G_{1})|\geq|V|-1

(38)

and we have
x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0
for every edge
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{1})
. Recall that each edge in
E\setminus E(G_{1})
has
x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
and as a result, the solution
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
has
|E\setminus E(G_{1})|
tight constraints among the last
|E|
rows (denoted by
S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))
). Recall that
\mathbf{x}
is a basic feasible solution and it has exactly
|E|
tight constraints (by Definition
D.2
). In particular, this implies there are
|E|-|E\setminus E(G_{1})|=|E(G_{1})|
more tight constraints. Further, note that these constraints are of the form

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\sum}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}=1

for some subset of vertices in
V
. We note these tight constraints by
S(G_{1})
and it follows that
|S(G_{1})|=|E(G_{1})|
(as discussed above).

Let
S=S(G_{1})\cup S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))
denote the set of all such tight constraints. Consider the matrix
C_{S}
, which is matrix
C
projected down to constraints (i.e., rows) in
S
. By definition of the basic feasible solution,
C_{S}
has rank
|E|
and we claim that
C_{S}
can be written as

\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}C_{S(G_{1})}&\mathbf{0}\\
\underbrace{\mathbf{0}}_{E(G_{1})}&\underbrace{C_{S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))}}_{E\setminus E(G_{1})}\end{array}\right)

Here,
C_{S(G_{1})}
and
C_{S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))}
denote the submatrices corresponding to tight constraints in
S(G_{1})
and
S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))
respectively. The block structure above follows from the fact that the edges in
E\setminus E(G_{1})
do not belong in
E(G_{1})
(by construction) and as a result, the constraints in
S(G_{1})
do not contain edges in
E\setminus E(G_{1})
. Further, by definition of constraints in
S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))
,
C_{S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))}
forms an identity matrix, resulting in

\displaystyle C_{S}=\begin{bmatrix}C_{S(G_{1})}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&I_{S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, we can write

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}(C_{S})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S(G_{1})}\right)+\mathrm{rank}(I_{S(E\setminus E(G_{1}))})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S(G_{1})}\right)+|E\setminus E(G_{1})|.

Since
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S})=|E|
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S(G_{1})}\right)=|E|-|E\setminus E(G_{1})|=|E(G_{1})|.

Further,
C_{S(G_{1})}
is a
|S(G_{1})|\times|E(G_{1})|
matrix, which implies

\displaystyle|E(G_{1})|=\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S(G_{1})}\right)\leq\min(|S(G_{1})|,|E(G_{1})|)\leq|S(G_{1})|\leq|V|.

Combining this with (
38
), we get

\displaystyle|V|-1\leq|E(G_{1})|\leq|V|,

as required. Since
\mathbf{x}_{1}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{1})}
is a feasible solution to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{1}
, where
|E(G_{1})|
of them (of the following kind) are tight:

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{1})}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)+\left(\sum_{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E(G_{1})}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}\right)>1,

which follows from the fact that
\mathbf{x}
was basic feasible to start with. Further, the matrix
C_{S(G_{1})}
has rank
E(G_{1})
, as shown earlier. Thus, we have that
\mathbf{x}_{1}
is basic feasible.

When there are multiple components, we consider each (undirected) connected component individually after edges satisfying
x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
are removed. Since the (undirected) connected components are pairwise vertex disjoint (if not, we can collapse them into a single connected component, again in the undirected sense), the connected components are maximal. Applying a similar argument as above, we would have that the matrices
C_{S(G_{i})}
(along with
I_{S(E\setminus\underset{i\in[t]}{\cup}E(G_{i}))}
) are all block diagonal and we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}(C_{S})

\displaystyle=\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}\left(\mathrm{rank}((C_{S(G_{i})})\right)+\mathrm{rank}\left(I_{S(E\setminus\underset{i\in[t]}{\cup}E(G_{i}))}\right),

which in turn implies (using
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S})=|E|
)

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}\left(\mathrm{rank}(C_{S(G_{i})})\right)

\displaystyle=|E|-|E\setminus\underset{i\in[t]}{\cup}E(G_{i})|

\displaystyle=\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}E(G_{i}).

Since
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S(G_{i})})\leq\min(|E(G_{i})|,|V(G_{i})|)
(by definition), the above implies
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S(G_{i})})=|E(G_{i})|
for every
i\in[t]
. To complete the proof, we argue that the solution
\mathbf{x}_{i}=\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}
is basic feasible for
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. In particular, there are
|E(G_{i})|
variables and
|V(G_{i})|
constraints in it, of which
|E(G_{i})|
are tight. The remaining
|V(G_{i})|-|E(G_{i})|
constraints are of the form

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)+\left(\sum_{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E(G_{i})}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}\right)>1,

which follows from (basic) feasibility of
\mathbf{x}
. Thus, we have shown that
\mathbf{x}_{i}
is a feasible solution to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
and it is basic feasible since
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S(G_{i})})=|E(G_{i})|
. This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we extend this result to the case when we are given both
\ell_{p}
(for a fixed
p
) and
\ell_{\infty}
constraints for the same relation as well.

D.3
Proof of Corollary
H.4

We restate
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
for this case and Corollary
H.4
before proving the latter.

\displaystyle\min\left(\underset{(v,u)\in E}{\sum}x_{({v},{u})}\log(L)+\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\log(d)\right)

(
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
)

\displaystyle\text{ s.t. }\left(\underset{e=(v,u)\ni u}{\sum}x_{v,u}\right)+\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)\geq 1\quad\forall u\in V

(39)

\displaystyle x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\geq 0\quad\forall({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E.

(40)

Corollary D.5
.

For any
G=(V,E)
, there exists an optimal solution
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})=(x^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G
that can be decomposed into a disjoint union of
t
(some
t>0
) connected components (in the undirected sense)
G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i})
with

\displaystyle|V_{i}|-1\leq|Q(E(G_{i}))|\leq|V_{i}|,\text{ where }

\displaystyle Q(E(G_{i}))=\{x_{({v},{u})}:x_{({v},{u})}\neq 0,({v},{u})\in E(G_{i})\}\cup\{z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}:z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\neq 0,({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})\}.

and
(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i},\mathbf{z}^{*}_{i})=\left(x_{({v},{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v},{u})\in E(G_{i})}
is an optimal basic feasible solution for
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. Further, we have
\cup_{i=1}^{t}V(G_{i})=V
and
V(G_{i})\cap V(G_{j})=\emptyset

\forall i,j\in[t],i\neq j
. The following is true:

\displaystyle\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}

\displaystyle=\times_{i\in[t]}\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(G_{i}).

Before proving this corollary, we first state an extremal property on optimal basic feasible solutions for each cyclic
G_{i}:i\in[t]
(from Corollary
H.4
).

Property D.6
.

Every optimal basic feasible solution

(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i},\mathbf{z}^{*}_{i})=(x^{*}_{({v},{u})},z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v},{u})\in E(G_{i})}

to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}:i\in[t]
is such that for every cyclic
G_{i}
, exactly one of
x^{*}_{({v},{u})}
(or)
z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
is non-zero for every (undirected) edge
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})
.

Proof of Property
D.6
.

Among all optimal basic feasible solutions (OBFS)
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G
, we are ruling out every OBFS, where

\displaystyle\exists({v},{u}),({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})\text{ s.t. }x^{*}_{({v},{u})}=0\text{ and }z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0.

For the remaining OBFS, we have

\displaystyle\forall({v},{u}),({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i}),x^{*}_{({v},{u})}>0\text{ or }z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0.

Note that there always exists a OBFS of this kind using Corollary
H.4
and as a result, the property can always be satisfied.
∎

We start by stating the corresponding version of Lemma
D.4
for this case.

Lemma D.7
.

For any
G=(V,E)
and for every basic feasible solution
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})=\left(x_{({v},{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}
to
LP^{(+)}
on
G
, there exists a
t
such that
G
can be decomposed into a disjoint union of
t
connected components (in the undirected sense)
G_{i}=(V_{i},E_{i})
such that
|V_{i}|-1\leq|E_{i}|\leq|V_{i}|
and
(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{i})=\left(x_{({v},{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E_{i}}
is a basic feasible solution to
LP^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. Further, we have
\cup_{i=1}^{t}V(G_{i})=V
and
V(G_{i})\cap V(G_{j})=\emptyset

\forall i,j\in[t],i\neq j
.

Assuming the above lemma is true, we can do the same proof as the Proof of Theorem
3.2
to complete the proof (and is omitted). We would like to mention here that the proof of Lemma
D.7
is very similar to the proof of Lemma
D.4
.

Proof of Lemma
D.7
.

We start by recalling the constraints of
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G
:

\displaystyle\left(\underset{e=(v,u)\ni u}{\sum}x_{v,u}\right)+\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)\geq 1\quad\forall u\in V

\displaystyle x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}\geq 0\quad\forall({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E

We define the
(|V|+2\cdot|E|)\times(2\cdot|E|)
constraint matrix
C
as follows (in the order of the constraints). The first
|V|
rows are indexed by vertices in
V
and the next
2\cdot|E|
rows are indexed by variables in lexicographic order in the LP. The columns are lexicographically indexed by variables of the LP i.e.,
(x_{({v},{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v},{u})\in E}
, consistent with the order of
2\cdot|E|
rows discussed above. For every
u\in V
, note that
C\left[u,x_{({v},{u})}\right]=C\left[u,z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right]=1
and
C\left[u,x_{({u},{w})}\right]=1
, where
v,w\in V
and
({v},{u}),({u},{w})\in E

29
29

29

Note here that for a given
u
, we have a non-zero entry in only
C[u,z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}]=1
(and not
C[v,z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}]
).
. For every edge
({v},{u})\in E
, note that
C\left[({v},{u}),x_{({v},{u})}\right]=1,C\left[({v}\rightarrow{u}),z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right]=1
. All the remaining entries in
C
are
0
.

Consider any basic feasible solution
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})=\left(x_{({v},{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v},{u})\in E}
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
. We remove every edge
({v},{u})\in E
from
G
such that
x_{({v},{u})}=z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
. Note that this process does not remove any vertex from
G
since

\displaystyle\left(\underset{e=(v,u)\ni u}{\sum}x_{v,u}\right)+\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)\geq 1

for every
u\in V
(by definition of
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{z}
).

Let the resulting graph after this process be
G_{1}
and for simplicity, we assume
G_{1}
is a single connected component in the undirected sense (else we consider the components individually), which implies

\displaystyle|E(G_{1})|\geq|V|-1

(41)

and we have either
x_{({v},{u})}>0
or
z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0
for every edge
({v},{u})\in E(G_{1})
. Recall that each edge in
E\setminus E(G_{1})
has
x_{({v},{u})}=z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
and as a result, the solution
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
has
2\cdot|E\setminus E(G_{1})|
tight constraints for each edge not in
E(G_{1})
and we denote this set of tight constraints by
S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(E\setminus E(G_{1}))
. Since
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
is a basic feasible solution and it has exactly
2\cdot|E|
tight constraints. In particular, this implies there are exactly

\displaystyle 2\cdot|E|-2\cdot|E\setminus E(G_{1})|=2\cdot|E(G_{1})|

more tight constraints. We argue that
\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)\leq 2\cdot|E(G_{1})|
, where the submatrix
C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}
corresponds to the tight constraints in
E(G_{1})

We note that the tight constraints in
C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}
can be of two types: the first one is of the form

\displaystyle\left(\underset{e=(v,u)\ni u}{\sum}x_{v,u}\right)+\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)=1

for some subset of vertices in
V
. We denote these tight constraints by
S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})
. The second type is of the form
x_{({v},{u})}=0
or
z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
(both cannot be true simultaneously since we would have removed the edge when that is the case), which we denote by
S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})
. Note that by definition, for each edge
({v},{u})\in E(G_{1})
, we have either
x_{({v},{u})}>0
(or)
z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0
. It follows that
|S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})|+|S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})|=2\cdot|E(G_{1})|
(as discussed above).

Consider the matrix
C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}
, which is
C
projected down to constraints in
S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})\cup S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})
. By definition of the basic feasible solution,
C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}
can be written as

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\\
C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\end{array}\right).

Note that this implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)\leq\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)+\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)

(42)

Recall that
S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})
contains only constraints of the form
x_{({v},{u})}=0
(or)
z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
for a subset of edges
({v},{u})\in E
and as a result,
C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}
forms an identity matrix.
Since
\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)=2\cdot|E(G_{1})|
(since
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
is a basic feasible solution) and

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)=|S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})|=2\cdot|E(G_{1})|-|Q(E(G_{1}))|,

which follows from our definition of
|Q(E(G_{1}))|
. More specifically there are
2\cdot|E(G_{1})|
variables
x_{({v},{u})}

z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
over all
({u},{v})\in E(G_{1})
and the set of non-zero ones (which cannot result in tight constraints in
S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})
) are exactly captured by
Q(E(G_{1}))
. Thus, (
42
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq 2\cdot|E(G_{1})|-\left(2\cdot|E(G_{1})|-|Q(E(G_{1}))|\right)

\displaystyle=|Q(E(G_{1}))|.

Since
C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}
is at most a
|V|\times|S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})|
matrix, we have

\displaystyle|Q(E(G_{1}))|\leq\mathrm{rank}\left(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})}\right)\leq\min\left(|V|,|S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{1})|\right)\leq|V|.

(43)

Finally, we have

\displaystyle|Q(E(G_{1}))|\geq|E(G_{1})|\geq|V|-1,

(44)

where the first inequality follows from the definition of
Q(E(G_{1}))
as a union of sets
\{x_{({v},{u})}:x_{({v},{u})}\neq 0,({v},{u})\in E(G_{i})\}
and
\{z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}:z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\neq 0,({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})\}
, which in turn implies that every edge
({u},{v})\in E(G_{1})
gets counted at least once in
Q(E(G_{1}))
(either as
x_{({u},{v})}\neq 0
or
z_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}\neq 0
).
In particular, we can now combine (
44
) with (
43
) to show that

\displaystyle|V|-1\leq|Q(E(G_{1}))|\leq|V|.

Note that (
44
) implies there exists at most one edge
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E_{1}
such that
both

x_{({u},{v})}\neq 0
and
z_{({u}\rightarrow{v})}\neq 0
.

When there are multiple components
G_{i}:i\in[t]
, we consider each (undirected) connected component individually after edges
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
of the form
x_{({v},{u})}=z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=0
are removed from
G
. Note that the (undirected) connected components are pairwise vertex-disjoint (if not, we can collapse them into one connected component in the undirected sense) and as a result, the connected components we obtained are maximal. Applying a similar argument as above, we would have that the matrices
C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})}
and
C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})}
for every
i\in[t]
(along with
I_{S(E\setminus\underset{i\in[t]}{\cup}E(G_{i}))}
) are all block diagonal and we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{rank}(C_{S})

\displaystyle=\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}\left(\mathrm{rank}(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})})+\mathrm{rank}(C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})})\right)+\mathrm{rank}\left(I_{S(E\setminus\underset{i\in[t]}{\cup}E(G_{i}))}\right),

which in turn implies (using
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S})=2|E|
and
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S^{2}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})})=2|E(G_{i})|-|Q(E(G_{i}))|
)

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}\left(\mathrm{rank}(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})})\right)

\displaystyle=2|E|-|E\setminus\underset{i\in[t]}{\cup}E(G_{i})|-\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}\left(2|E(G_{i})|-|Q(E(G_{i}))|\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{i\in[t]}{\sum}|Q(E(G_{i}))|.

Since
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})})\leq\min(|Q(E(G_{i}))|,|V(G_{i})|)
(by definition), the above implies
\mathrm{rank}(C_{S^{1}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})})=|Q(E(G_{i}))|
for every
i\in[t]
.

To complete the proof, we argue that the solution
(\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{z}_{i})=\left(x_{({v},{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}
is basic feasible for
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
. Note that there are
2\cdot|E(G_{i})|
variables and
|V(G_{i})|+2\cdot|E(G_{i})|
constraints in it of which
|Q(E(G_{i}))|
are tight i.e., we have either

\displaystyle\left(\underset{e\ni u:e=(v,u)\in E}{\sum}x_{v,u}\right)+\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G_{i})}{\sum}z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)>1\text{ for some }u\in V

\displaystyle\text{ (or) }x_{({v},{u})}>0

\displaystyle\text{ (or) }z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0,

which follows from basic feasibility of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})
. Thus, we have shown that
(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{z}_{i})
is a feasible solution to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
and it is basic feasible since
C_{S_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z}}(G_{i})}
has rank exactly
2\cdot|E(G_{1})|
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We first prove the following result using Algorithm
2.2
(without assuming anything about its runtime):

\left|{\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})}\right|=|J_{n}|\leq\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{d},G).

(45)

In particular, we will argue that for each
i\in[n]
and
\mathbf{t}\in J_{i-1}
, we have at the end of the iteration
i
:

\displaystyle\left|{P_{i}(\mathbf{t})}\right|\leq\mathcal{D}_{u_{i}}(\mathbf{d}).

Note that the above combined with the defnition of
P_{i}(\mathbf{t})
implies

\displaystyle|J_{i}|\leq\underset{(u_{1},\dots u_{i})}{\prod}\mathcal{D}_{u_{i}}(\mathbf{d}).

When
i=n
, we have

\displaystyle|J_{n}|\leq\underset{u\in V}{\prod}\mathcal{D}_{u}(\mathbf{d}).

Recall that the RHS is exactly
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{d},G
).

To prove (
45
), it suffices (by definition of
\mathcal{D}_{u_{i}}(\mathbf{d})
) to prove for every
i\in[n]
and
\mathbf{t}\in\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}_{i-1}
:

\left|{P_{\text{in}}(i,\mathbf{t})}\right|\leq\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u_{i}})\in E}{\min}d_{({v}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}

(46)

and

\left|{P_{\text{out}}(i)}\right|\leq\underset{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\min}\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{p}}{d_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}.

(47)

Indeed, (
46
) just follows from the definition of the degree configuration
\mathbf{d}
. Finally, (
47
) follows by applying Lemma
C.3
on each edge
({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})\in E
.

Next, we argue the correctness of our algorithm by showing that
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})=J_{n}=\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Let
J_{n}\subset\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Note that this implies there exists a tuple
\mathbf{t}\in\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
such that
\mathbf{t}\notin J_{n}
. Based on Algorithm
2.2
, this would imply there exists at least one attribute
u_{i}:i\in[n]
such that
\pi_{u_{i}}(\mathbf{t})\notin P_{i}(\mathbf{t}_{u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}})
, which implies
\mathbf{t}\notin\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
, contradicting our earlier assumption. To complete the argument, we argue the reverse as well i.e.,
J_{n}\supset\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Assume there exists a tuple
\mathbf{t}\in J_{n}
such that
\mathbf{t}\notin\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
. Note that there exists at least one attribute
u_{i}:i\in[n]
and a relation
R_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot})}^{d_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot})}}/R_{({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}^{d_{({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}}
for some
({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot}),({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})\in E
such that
\pi_{u_{i}}(\mathbf{t})\not\in\pi_{u_{i}}\left(R_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot})}^{d_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot})}}\right),\pi_{u_{i}}\left(R_{({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}^{d_{({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}}\right)
. This results in a contradiction since
P_{i}(\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{u_{1},\dots,u_{i-1}})
(by definition) for any tuple
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in J_{i-1}
has only values from
\pi_{u_{i}}\left(R_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot})}^{d_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{\cdot})}}\right)
and
\pi_{u_{i}}\left(R_{({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}^{d_{({\cdot}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}}\right)
. Thus, we have
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}(\mathbf{d})=J_{n}=\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\bowtie}R^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
, as required.

Finally, we argue that Algorithm
2.2
runs in time
O(\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{d},G))
. In order to do this, we argue that for every
i\in[n]
and
\mathbf{t}\in J_{i-1}
,
\left|{P_{i}(\mathbf{t})}\right|
(defined above) can computed in time
O(|E|\cdot\left|{P_{i}(\mathbf{t})}\right|)
and the final runtime follows as a result. Recall that each relation is stored in a two level B-tree-like index structure consistent with the topological ordering
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
. In particular, for any relation
R_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{u_{j}})}
(with
j>i
), the first level of its B-tree is indexed by
(u_{i})
(i.e., all values ) and the second level is indexed by
(u_{j},\mathrm{val}_{u_{j}})
, where
\mathrm{val}_{u_{j}}\in\mathrm{Dom}(u_{j})
. As a result, for a fixed
i\in[n]
and
\mathbf{t}\in J_{i-1}
, note that we can access the set

\displaystyle S_{(\mathbf{t}[v],u_{i})}=\left\{y:(\mathbf{t}[v],y)\in R_{({v}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}^{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u_{i}})}}\right\}

directly using the B-tree index on
(v,\mathbf{t}[v])
and similarly, we can access the set

\displaystyle S_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})}=\pi_{u_{i}}\left(R_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})}^{d_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})}}\right)

directly using the B-tree index on
(u_{i})
. In particular, we are now computing a set intersection on sorted sublists

\displaystyle(S_{(\mathbf{t}[v],u_{i})},S_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})}),

whose size

\displaystyle\left|\left(\cap_{(\mathbf{t}[v],u_{i}):({v}\rightarrow{u_{i}})\in E}S_{(\mathbf{t}[v],u_{i})}\right)\cap\left(\cap_{({u_{i}}\rightarrow{w})\in E}S_{(u_{i},w)}\right)\right|

(48)

is exactly
|P_{i}(\mathbf{t})|
. Since (
48
) can be computed in time
|E|\cdot|P_{i}(\mathbf{t})|
(where recall that we are working in the RAM model), this completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma

.

Invoking Theorem
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{d},G)

\displaystyle=\underset{u\in V}{\prod}\min\left((d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E},\left(\frac{2^{p}\cdot L_{{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}\right)_{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}\right)

(49)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p|V|}\underset{u\in V}{\prod}\min\left((d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E},\left(\frac{L_{{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}\right)_{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p|V|}\cdot\underset{u\in V}{\prod}\left(\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\prod}d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right)\cdot\left(\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\prod}\left(\frac{L_{{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}\right)^{\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}}\right)\right)

(50)

\displaystyle=2^{p|V|}\cdot\underset{u\in V}{\prod}\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\prod}d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\cdot d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\cdot\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\prod}\frac{L_{{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}}\right)

\displaystyle=2^{p|V|}\cdot\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\prod}\left(d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\cdot L_{({v}\rightarrow{u},{\mathbf{d}})}^{x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right).

(51)

In the above, (
49
) follows by definition of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{d},G)
. We argue (
50
) next. Note that

\displaystyle\min\left((d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E},\left(\frac{L_{{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}\right)_{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\prod}d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}^{x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}\right)\cdot\left(\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\prod}\left(\frac{L_{{({u}\rightarrow{w},{\mathbf{d}})}}^{p}}{d_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}^{p}}\right)^{\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}}\right)\quad\forall u\in V

(52)

for any
\left((x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E},(x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})})_{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}\right)
such that

•

x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})},z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\geq 0
for every
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
follows from (
7
).

•

\displaystyle\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}\left(x_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)+\underset{({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E}{\sum}\frac{x_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}}{p}\geq 1.

(53)

Note that this is the same as (
6
).

We can now invoke Lemma
C.1
to get (

). Further, (

) follows by noting that since

is a DAG, each edge
({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E
occurs exactly twice – once as a bound of

and the other as a bound for

(both following from Theorem

). As a result, we can cancel the

term to get (
