.

H.2.2
Proof of (
14
)

We make the following claim.

Lemma H.5
.

Each
G_{i}:i\in[t]
is a DAG.

If the above result holds, note that we can invoke Theorem
4.3
on each
G_{i}
to prove (
14
). The rest of this section will focus on proving Lemma
H.5
and we assume
G_{i}
is cyclic and since
|V(G_{i})|-1\leq|E(G_{i})\leq|V(G_{i})|
, we have that it has exactly one cycle, which we denote by
C
.

H.2.3
Proof of Lemma
H.5

We make the following claim on
G_{i}
on
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
.

Claim H.6
.

For some
k\geq 2
, there exists a path (in the undirected sense)
P=\{u,\{v_{i}\}_{i\in[k-1]},w\}
such that (for all
\in[k-1]
and
u,v_{1}\in V(C)
)

\displaystyle x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}>0,z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}>0,z^{*}_{({v_{k}}\rightarrow{w})}>0.

Further, for edges
({u},{v_{1}})
and
({w},{v_{k}})
, we have
x_{({u},{v_{1}})}=x_{({w},{v_{k}})}=0
and for every
({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}}):i\in[t]
, we have
z_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}=0
.

Assuming the above claim is true, we prove Lemma
H.5
by a contradiction. In particular, we will construct an alternative optimal solution to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G_{i}
with one of the following properties:

Property H.7
.

When
|E(P)|
is odd (i.e.,
k
is even), our solution has a smaller objective value than the optimal solution
(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i},\mathbf{z}^{*}_{i})
we started with, contradicting the optimality of
(\mathbf{x}^{*}_{i},\mathbf{z}^{*}_{i})
.

Property H.8
.

When
|E(P)|
is even (i.e.,
k
is odd), there exists an optimal basic feasible solution
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
and at least one edge
({v},{u})\in E
such that
x^{\prime}_{({v},{u})}=z^{\prime}_{({v},{u})}=0
, contradicting Assumption
D.6
.

As discussed above, in both cases, we would end up in a contradiction, proving Lemma
H.5
, as required.

In the remaining section, we will sketch the proofs of Properties
H.7
and
H.8
. Let
\epsilon
be defined as the minimum of the following two expressions:

\displaystyle\min\left(z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},1-z_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})},1-z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})\in E(P),i\in[k-1]}{\min}\left(x^{*}_{({v},{u})},1-x^{*}_{({v},{u})}\right)

The following results are true:

\displaystyle z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}\pm\epsilon\in[0,1],z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}\pm\epsilon\in[0,1]

(126)

\displaystyle x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}\pm\epsilon\in[0,1]\quad\forall i\in[k-1]

(127)

and at least one of the following is true:

\displaystyle z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=\epsilon\text{ (or) }z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=1-\epsilon

\displaystyle x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=\epsilon\text{ (or) }x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=1-\epsilon\text{ for some }i\in[k-1]

\displaystyle z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=\epsilon\text{ (or) }z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v})}=1-\epsilon.

Here (
126
), (
127
) and the above follow by the definition of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})

and
\epsilon
.

We construct two alternative solutions, starting with some notation for all
i\in[k-1]
and
\alpha\in[-1,1]
:

\displaystyle x^{\alpha}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}+\alpha

and

\displaystyle z^{\alpha}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+\alpha,

\displaystyle z^{\alpha}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+\alpha

The first solution is where we decrease
z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}
by
\epsilon
and subsequently, increase
x_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}
by
\epsilon
and so on. In particular, we would have a solution of the form

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},\left(x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}\right)_{i\in[k-1]},z^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}.

(128)

The second solution is where we increase
z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}
by
\epsilon
and we have a solution of the form.

\displaystyle z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},\left(x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}\right)_{i\in[k-1]},z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}.

(129)

We claim the following based on (
128
) and (
129
).

Claim H.9
.

The constraint (
125
) is tight on each vertex in
P=(u,v_{1},\dots,v_{k},w)
in both (
128
) and (
129
).

Assuming the above claim is true (the proof is in Appendix
H.2.6
), we sketch the proofs of Properties
H.7
and
H.8
.
It turns out that when
|E(P)|
is odd, (
128
) has a strictly smaller objective value than
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
, resulting in a contradiction. When
|E(P)|
is even, there exists at least one edge with value
0
in one of (
128
) and (
129
), which contradicts an extremal property of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
.

H.2.4
Proof of Properties
H.7
and
H.8

In this section, our goal is to prove Properties
H.7
and
H.8
. We recall some notation
P=\{u,v_{1},\dots,v_{k},w\}
for all
i\in[k-1]
:

\displaystyle x^{-\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}-\epsilon,x^{+\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}+\epsilon

and

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}-\epsilon,z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+\epsilon

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}-\epsilon,z^{+\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+\epsilon,

where
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
is an optimal basic feasible solution to
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G
. We restate (
128
) below.

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},\left(x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}\right)_{i\in[k-1]},z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}.

(130)

Note that the remaining values are the same as
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
.

Next, we compute the ratio of the new objective value and
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\underset{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})\in E\setminus E(P)}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})}}d^{z^{*}_{({f_{1}}\rightarrow{f_{2}})}}\right)\cdot d^{z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{\left(\underset{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})\in E\setminus E(P)}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})}}\cdot d^{z^{*}_{({f_{1}}\rightarrow{f_{2}})}}\right)d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{-\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}\quad\text{ when }k\text{ is even}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}-\epsilon}d^{z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}-\epsilon}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{L}{d^{2}}\right)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle<1

where the final inequality follows from
d^{2}>L
. This proves Property
H.7
since the bound obtained by (
128
) is strictly better than that of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
, resulting in a contradiction.

We now consider the case when
k
is odd, where we have

\displaystyle\frac{d^{z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{+\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=1.

(131)

Note that the objective values are the same in this case. We do a similar computation for (
129
) as well, this time starting with the case when
k
is odd and first restate (
129
).

\displaystyle z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},\left(x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}\right)_{i\in[k-1]},z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}.

(132)

\displaystyle\frac{\left(\underset{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})\in E\setminus E(P)}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})}}d^{z^{*}_{({f_{1}}\rightarrow{f_{2}})}}\right)\cdot d^{z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{\left(\underset{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})\in E\setminus E(P)}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({f_{1}},{f_{2}})}}d^{z^{*}_{({f_{1}}\rightarrow{f_{2}})}}\right)d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+\epsilon}d^{z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}-\epsilon}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=1.

We are now ready to argue Property
H.8
based on (
130
) and (
132
). Recall the definition of
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle\epsilon=\min\left\{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},1-z_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})},1-z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})},\underset{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})\in E(P),i\in[k-1]}{\min}\left\{x^{*}_{({v},{u})},1-x^{*}_{({v},{u})}\right\}\right\}.

WLOG let
x^{*}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}
be the value achieving the
\epsilon
minimum. Then, one of the following conditions is true:

\displaystyle x^{*}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}=\epsilon

\displaystyle x^{*}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}=1-\epsilon.

Assuming the first case, we would have added it by
\epsilon
in (
130
) and subtracted it by
\epsilon
in (
132
). The latter would give us
x^{-\epsilon}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}=0
, contradicting Property
D.6
. Now, consider the second case, where we would have added it by
\epsilon
in (
132
) and subtracted it by
\epsilon
in (
130
). In the former, we would have
x^{+\epsilon}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}=1
, which in turn implies
x^{-\epsilon}_{({v_{2}},{v_{3}})}=0
and
z^{-\epsilon}_{({u},{v_{1}})}=0
. This contradicts Property
D.6
as well and proves Property
H.8
, as required.

For the sake of completenes, we consider the case when
k
is even as well. However, it turns out that the objective value in this case is worse than that of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
and as a result, we can ignore it.

\displaystyle\frac{d^{z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+\epsilon}d^{z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+\epsilon}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}}\rightarrow{v_{i+1}})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}}\underset{i\in[k-1]}{\prod}L^{x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}}}\quad\text{ when }k\text{ is even}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d^{2}}{L}\right)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle>1.

This completes the proof.

H.2.5
Proof of Claim
H.6

We prove Claim
H.6
here. Our goal here is to argue that for some
k\geq 2
, there exists a path
P=\{u,v_{1},\dots,v_{k},w\}
such that
u,v_{1}\in V(C),z_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}>0
,
z^{*}_{({v_{k}}\rightarrow{w})}>0
and
x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}>0
for every
i\in[k-1]
.

We first claim that there always exists a path
P^{\prime}=\{u,v_{1},v_{2}\}
in
G
such that
u,v_{1}\in V(C),z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}>0,x^{*}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}>0
. Note that
P^{\prime}
is a subpath of
P
.

Assuming the above claim is true, we construct
P
from
P^{\prime}
as follows. Consider the vertex
v_{2}
. If it has an incoming edge
({w}\rightarrow{v_{2}})
with
z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{2}})}>0
and we have constructed a
P=\{P^{\prime},w\}
, as required. If not, there are two possibilities –
(1)

v_{2}
has only outgoing edges
({v_{2}}\rightarrow{y})\in E
with
z^{*}_{({v_{2}}\rightarrow{y})}>0
.
(2)

v_{2}
at least one more incident edge
({v_{2}},{v_{2}})\in E
with
x^{*}_{({v_{2}},{v_{3}})}>0
.

In case of
(1)
, note that we have
x^{*}_{({v_{1}}\rightarrow{v_{2}})}=1
(by basic feasibility of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
) and this would violate the tightness of the constraint on
v_{1}
i.e., we have

\displaystyle z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{1}}\rightarrow{v_{2}})}>1,

contradicting our assumption that
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
is basic feasible. If we fall back to
(2)
, then we update
P=P^{\prime}\cup\{v_{3}\}
and continue this process. There are two stopping conditions for this procedure –
(3)
we identify a
w
as above and terminate with a valid
P
and
(4)
the
v_{k}
for some
k>2
we hit in our procedure is a leaf (i.e., has degree
1
) in
G
. We show that
(4)
cannot happen, in particular, if
v_{k}
is a leaf, then it is true that
x^{*}_{({v_{k-1}}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=1
(again by basic feasibility of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
) and this would violate the tightness of the constraint on
v_{k-1}
i.e., we have

\displaystyle x^{*}_{({v_{k-2}}\rightarrow{v_{k-1}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{k-1}}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}>1,

contradicting the basic feasibility of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
.As a result, we can always construct a valid
P
as required.

To complete the proof, we reason about the cases where no such path of the kind
P^{\prime}
exists.

•

The obvious case is when the graph
G
is a cycle
C
and all edges
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E(G)
have
z_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}>0
.

•

All vertices in the cycle
C
have only outgoing edges
({u}\rightarrow{w})\in E(G),u\in V(C),w\not\in V(C)
with
z_{({u}\rightarrow{w})}>0
.

•

There exists at least one vertex
u\in V(C)
with at least one incoming edge
({v}\rightarrow{u})
from outside the cycle i.e.,
v\not\in V(C)
.

We argue the first and second cases in one shot and argue the third case separately. For the first two cases,
we construct an alternative basic feasible solution
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
to
G
as follows. We pick an arbitrary edge
({v},{u})\in E(C)
and set
x^{\prime}_{({v},{u})}=1
and
z^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=z^{\prime}_{({w}\rightarrow{v})}=0
. The remaining values in
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
are the same as
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
Computing the ratio of objective values given by
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
for edges in
C
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{Ld^{|V(C)|-2}}{d^{|V(C)|}}

\displaystyle=\frac{L}{d^{2}}

\displaystyle<1,

where the inequality follows from
d^{2}>L
. Note that the remaining edges in
G
have the same value in
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{x}^{*}
(by definition of
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
). We now argue that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is basic feasible and to do so, we only need to focus on vertices
u
and
v
whose tightness could have been affected by our construction. Since we have
x^{\prime}_{({v},{u})}=1
and
v
,
u
can have only outgoing edges outside of
E(C)
, the constraints are still tight on
v
and
u
respectively. Thus,
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
is basic feasible and has a smaller objective value than
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
. As a result, we can replace
({v}\rightarrow{u})
with
({v},{u})
and drop the edge
({w}\rightarrow{v})
to make
G
a DAG.

Finally, we argue the third case. As in the argument above, we construct an alternative optimal solution
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
as follows. We focus on
({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E
such that
v\not\in V(C)
and
u\in V(C)
and consider
({w}\rightarrow{u})\in E(C)
. We set
z^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}
and
z^{\prime}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}=0
. The remaining values in
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
are the same as
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
Computing the ratio of objective values given by
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
and
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
for
G
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d^{z^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}}{d}^{z^{\prime}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}}{d^{z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}d^{z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{z^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}}}{d^{z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}}}

\displaystyle=1,

where the equation follows from definitions of
z^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}
and
z^{\prime}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}
. Note that the remaining edges in
G
have the same value in
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{x}^{*}
(by definition of
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
). We now argue that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is basic feasible and to do so, we only need to focus on vertices
u
whose tightness could have been affected by our construction. Since
z^{\prime}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}=z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}+z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{u})}
, the constraint is still tight on
u
. Thus, we have constructed a new solution
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
that is basic feasible, has the same objective value as
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
and contradicts Property
D.6
. As a result, a
G
of this kind cannot exist.

This completes the proof we since in all the above cases, we could always construct an alternative solution,
where
G
is DAG.

H.2.6
Proof of Claim
H.9

We recall some notation
P=\{u,v_{1},\dots,v_{k},w\}
for all
i\in[k-1]
:

\displaystyle x^{-\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}-\epsilon,x^{+\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}=x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}+\epsilon

and

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}-\epsilon,z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+\epsilon

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}-\epsilon,z^{+\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+\epsilon.

We recall the constraint (
125
) for every
u\in V
as well for an optimal basic feasible solution
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
:

\displaystyle\left(\underset{e=(v,u)\ni u}{\sum}x^{*}_{({v},{u})}\right)+\left(\underset{({v}\rightarrow{u})\in E}{\sum}z^{*}_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\right)=1\quad\forall u\in V,

(133)

where the tightness of the constraint follows from the basic feasibility of
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{z}^{*})
.

We prove this claim first for (
128
), followed by (
129
) and start by restating (
128
).

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},\left(x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}\right)_{i\in[k-1]},z^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}.

(134)

Note that the variables
z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}
and
z^{(-1)^{k-1}}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}
do not belong in (
125
) for
u
and
w
. As a result, the constraint (
125
) is still tight for
u
and
w
. Next, we consider the set of vertices
(v1,\dots,v_{k})
and for every
v_{i}:i\in[2,k-2]
, we have

\displaystyle x^{(-1)^{i-2}\epsilon}_{({v_{i-1}},{v_{i}})}+x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}

\displaystyle=x^{*}_{({v_{i-1}},{v_{i}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})},

where the equation follows from the fact that
(-1)^{i-2}
and
(-1)^{i-1}
have different parities for every
i\in[2,k-2]
. Since we didn’t modify values of the other edges incident on
v_{i}
and (
133
) was true for
v_{i}
to start with, our modification doesn’t affect the tightness. We can do a similar argument as above for
v_{1}
and
v_{k}
as well, where we have

\displaystyle z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+x^{\epsilon}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}

\displaystyle=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}

\displaystyle z^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+x^{(-1)^{k-2}\epsilon}_{({v_{k-1}},{v_{k}})}

\displaystyle=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{k-1}},{v_{k}})},

as required.

To complete the proof, we do a similar argument for (
129
) as well, starting by restating it.

\displaystyle z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})},\left(x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}\right)_{i\in[k-1]},z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}.

(135)

Similar to the earlier argument,
z^{-\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}
and
z^{(-1)^{k}}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}
do not belong in (
125
) for
u
and
w
. As a result, the constraint (
125
) is still tight for
u
and
w
. Next, we consider the set of vertices
(v1,\dots,v_{k})
and for every
v_{i}:i\in[2,k-2]
, we have

\displaystyle x^{(-1)^{i-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{i-1}},{v_{i}})}+x^{(-1)^{i}\epsilon}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})}

\displaystyle=x^{*}_{({v_{i-1}},{v_{i}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{i}},{v_{i+1}})},

where the equation follows from the fact that
(-1)^{i-1}
and
(-1)^{i}
have different parities for every
i\in[2,k-2]
. Since we didn’t modify values of the other edges incident on
v_{i}
and (
133
) was true for
v_{i}
to start with, our modification doesn’t affect the tightness. We can do a similar argument as above for
v_{1}
and
v_{k}
as well, where we have

\displaystyle z^{+\epsilon}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+x^{-\epsilon}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}

\displaystyle=z^{*}_{({u}\rightarrow{v_{1}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}

\displaystyle z^{(-1)^{k}\epsilon}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+x^{(-1)^{k-1}\epsilon}_{({v_{k-1}},{v_{k}})}

\displaystyle=z^{*}_{({w}\rightarrow{v_{k}})}+x^{*}_{({v_{k-1}},{v_{k}})},

as required. This completes the proof.

Appendix I
Extensions to Hypergraphs and other Open Questions

I.1
Extending our results to (acyclic) hypergraphs

In this section, we outline how your techniques (which have been so far only used for simple graphs) can be extended to work with hypergraphs. The aim of this section is not to present the most general result we can prove for hypergraphs but rather to show that the bottleneck in extending our arguments to the hypergraph case is
not
the notion of a degree configuration. Thus, to keep the notation simple(r), we will focus on the case of
\ell_{1}
bounds only and we outline how we can recover the AGM bound. We also note that by our we have a ‘proof from the book’ for the AGM bound and while the proof below can be considered ‘new’ we do not see potential benefit of the argument
other
than the fact that it shows that our degree configuration based bounds can be extended to hypergraphs. Finally, we only consider the problem of computing an upper bound on the size of the join output (the arguments for matching lower bound and the corresponding worst-case optimal algorithms follow along the usual lines but again for less clutter we omit those here).

Before we dive into our argument, we first define a generalization of notion of degrees and degree configuration for hypergraphs (the definitions below are also valid for the case of
\ell_{\infty}
bounds though we’ll not consider the latter here).

We now consider the case when
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
is a
hypergraph
and start by recalling the notion of

simple
degree constraints from
[
15
]
, where we have for any
subsets
X,Y\subseteq\mathcal{V}
of variables, a degree constraint
Y|X
is considered simple if
|X|\leq 1
. For our definition of degree constraint, we place an additional restriction that
X\cup Y=\mathcal{E}
as well. This would make our degree constraints guarded by the relation
R_{X\cup Y}
.

Definition I.1

(Simple and Guarded degree constraints for hypergraphs)
.

For every
F\in\mathcal{E}
and
u\in F
, we use
L_{(u\rightarrow F\setminus\left\{u\right\},\infty)}
to denote the degree constraint of
u
in
F
. More specifically we first define the degree of
a\in\mathrm{Dom}(u)
as

d_{({u}\rightarrow{F\setminus\left\{u\right\}})}[a]=\left|{\left\{\mathbf{t}|(a,\mathbf{t})\in R_{F}\right\}}\right|.

Then the degree constraint
L_{(u\rightarrow F\setminus\left\{u\right\},\infty)}
implies that for every
a\in\mathrm{Dom}(u)
:

d_{({u}\rightarrow{F\setminus\left\{u\right\}})}[a]\leq L_{(u\rightarrow F\setminus\left\{u\right\},\infty)}.

It will be useful to define a
set
of degree constrains that basically keeps track of the set of simple degree constraint:

Definition I.2
.

A set of degree constraints for hypergraph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
, is a subset
\mathcal{C}(G)\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{E}
. We now overload notation by also referring to
L_{(u\rightarrow F\setminus\left\{u\right\},\infty)}
as
L_{(u,F)}
.

Following
[
21
]
, we denote the corresponding constraints acyclic (and we overload notation and call
G
) to be acyclic if the simple graph obtained by replacing the constraint
({u}\rightarrow{F\setminus\left\{u\right\}})
by binary directed edges
\left\{({u}\rightarrow{v})|v\in F\setminus\left\{u\right\}\right\}
is acyclic.

Since
\ell_{1}
constraints do not have any inherent direction we impose such a direction. In particular, fix an ordering of vertices
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
in
\mathcal{V}
and for each edge
F\in\mathcal{E}
if
u
is the first vertex in
F
according to this order, then add
(u,F)
to
\mathcal{C}(G)
.

For the rest of the section fix
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})
and a set of degree constraints
\mathcal{C}(G)
(as defined above).
Recall we want to consider the setup where we have for each
F\in\mathcal{E}
, an
\ell_{1}
bound
N_{F}
(i.e. size of
R_{F}
). We now (re)consider the AGM/edge cover LP for hypergraphs:

\displaystyle\min\underset{F\in\mathcal{E}}{\sum}x_{F}\cdot\log(N_{F})

(
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
)

\displaystyle\underset{u\ni F\in\mathcal{E}}{\sum}x_{F}\geq 1\quad\forall u\in V

(136)

\displaystyle x_{F}\geq 0\quad\forall F\in E

We will argue that for any DB instance that satisfies the
\ell_{1}
bounds, we can upper bound the join output size by

\underset{F\in\mathcal{E}}{\prod}\left(N_{F}\right)^{x_{F}}.

We argue this by induction on the size of
G
.
As the base case consider the situation that
|\mathcal{V}|=1
. In this case we have the set intersection problem and it is known that the above bound is true (this e.g. follows from the fact that the size of the output is bounded by the size of smallest set (and then using Lemma
C.1
along with (
136
)).

Let us assume that for every
a\in\mathrm{Dom}(a)
, we have

d_{({u}\rightarrow{F\setminus\left\{u\right\}})}[a]=L_{(u,F)},

(137)

for some value
L_{(u,F)}
.
(We’ll later outline how we can get rid of this assumption.)

We’ll use notation equivalent to one used in
[
21
, Theorem 4.1]
(though in there the recursion happens in the reverse topological ordering):

\displaystyle\partial(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\left\{F|u_{1}\in F\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left\{F|F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}\text{ or }F\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\partial(u_{1})\wedge F\neq\emptyset\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}\setminus\left\{u_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{V}^{\prime},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}(G)\setminus\left\{(u_{1},F)|(u_{1},F)\in\mathcal{C}(G)\right\}

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{F}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}R_{F}&\text{ if }F\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\partial(u_{1})\\
\pi_{F}\left(R_{F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}}\right)&F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}\end{cases}

\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

\displaystyle N^{\prime}_{F}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}N_{F}&\text{ if }F\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\partial(u_{1})\\
L_{(u_{1},F)}&F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}\end{cases}

\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{F}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x_{F}&\text{ if }F\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\partial(u_{1})\\
x_{F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}}&F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}\end{cases}

\displaystyle F\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}

Note that by arguments similar to those in Section
4
the number of choices of
a\in\mathrm{Dom}(u_{1})
that can be in the output is upper bounded by

\min_{u_{1}\ni F\in\mathcal{E}}\left\{\frac{L_{F}}{L_{(u,F)}}\right\}\leq\prod_{u_{1}\ni F\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\frac{L_{F}}{L_{(u,F)}}\right)^{x_{F}}.

(138)

Note that for each such choice of
a
for
u_{1}
above, we have an instance of the join problem on
G^{\prime}
(as defined above) and by induction hypothesis the size of each of these instance is upper bounded by (note that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
is indeed a feasible solution for
\mathrm{LP}^{(+)}
on
G^{\prime}
):

\underset{F\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}{\prod}\left(N^{\prime}_{F}\right)^{x^{\prime}_{F}}=\underset{F\in\mathcal{E}\setminus\partial(u_{1})}{\prod}\left(N^{\prime}_{F}\right)^{x^{\prime}_{F}}\cdot\underset{F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}\in\mathcal{E}}{\prod}\left(L_{(u_{1},F)}\right)^{x_{F\cup\left\{u_{1}\right\}}}

(139)

Multiplying the bounds in (
138
) and (
139
), we re-prove the AGM bound as desired.

We quickly address the assumption in (
137
). First we get around the exact degree assumption by assuming that all degrees are within buckets of type
[2^{i},2^{i+1})
for some
i
. Again, if we assumed the final AGM bound for all degree configurations, we will incur an extra
O(\log^{|\mathcal{E}|}{N})
multiplicative factor. However, we can use the trick of ‘pushing’ the sum over all degree configuration inside the product using Hölder’s inequality as we have done in other cases.

We conclude this section by noting that if we ‘unroll’ the recursion above (as we have done implicitly in our arguments), then it turns out that for each
F\in\mathcal{E}
not only do we have to monitor the degree constraint
(u,F)
(where again
u
is the first according to some global ordering) but if say
F=(u=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell})
then we need to keep track of ‘degrees’ for
({u_{i}}\rightarrow{(u_{i+1},\dots,u_{\ell})})
for all
0\leq i\leq\ell-1
. We leave the question of working out the details as an tantalizing possibility for future work.

I.2
More Open Questions

We leave the following open questions for future and discuss current limitations to achieve them.

Question I.3
.

Can we generalize our results for same
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{\infty}
bounds for all edges to different
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{\infty}
bounds, potentially resolving Question
1.4
completely in affirmative for all simple graphs?

Our results in this regime currently rely on very specific structural decompositions based on the value of
d
. Extending them to different
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{\infty}
values could need more finer decompositions. Finally, we note that in most of the above cases, our degree configuration is more robust and extensible but our structural results are what cause the limitation.

Our assumption that we fix
p
upfront in Question
1.1
leads to the following question:

Question I.4
.

What is the optimal
p
that must be chosen for each relation for a given query and input data?

We would like to emphasize here that Algorithm
2.2
is
p
-agnostic and uses only a global ordering of attributes in the query. As a result, we conclude by conjecturing that the algorithmremains the same in both the above scenarios, while the analysis could be more technically involved.

Finally, we consider the following question.

Question I.5
.

Can we extend our results on simple graphs for
p\in[1,2]
to the case when
p>2
?

It turns out that when
p>2
, the optimal hard instances are not necessarily Cartesian product-based. To see this, consider the case when
p=3
,
G
is a triangle with a cyclic orientation and all
\ell_{3}
-norm bounds are upper bounded by
L
. The best Cartesian product based lower bound that can be achieved in this case is
L^{9/4}
(using our LP-based result). However, we can get a (trivial) lower bound of
L^{3}
on
|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}|
using the below instance:

\displaystyle||R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}||_{3}=||R_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}||_{3}=||R_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}||_{3}=\{(i,i):i\in[L^{3}]\}.

We present a slightly more formal result below and end with a conjecture, as we did for the hypergraph case.

I.2.1
Extending our results to
p\in[1,\infty]
for all simple graphs

In this section, we outline how some of our techniques for
p\in[1,\infty)
(which have so far been used only for graphs with girth at least
p+1
) can be extended to work for all simple graphs. The aim of this section is not to present the most general result we can prove for this case but rather to show the bottleneck in extending our arguments to the case of arbitrary simple graphs. To this end, we first fix
p>|V|-1
. We will focus on the case when
G
is a triangle with a cyclic orientation, all
\ell_{p}
-norm bounds are upper bounded by
L
and outline how we can prove tight bounds (up to a poly-logarithmic factor in
|E|
) in this case. We mainly consider the problem of proving an optimal lower bound on the size of the join output and briefly discuss a plan of attack for the upper bound.

We consider the problem of obtaining the best lower bound in this case when
G
is a triangle with a cyclic orientation. As we saw in Section
7
, our current Cartesian product based lower bounds do not hold when
p>2
. We define the following LP.

\displaystyle\max\quad y_{A}+y_{B}+y_{C}+m

(
\mathrm{LP}_{p}^{(**)}
)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }\frac{y_{A}}{p}+y_{B}+\frac{m}{p}\leq\log(L)

\displaystyle\frac{y_{B}}{p}+y_{C}+\frac{m}{p}\leq\log(L)

\displaystyle\frac{y_{C}}{p}+y_{A}+\frac{m}{p}\leq\log(L).

Consider an optimal solution
\mathbf{y}^{*}=(y^{*}_{A},y^{*}_{B},y^{*}_{C},m^{*})
to the LP above. We construct the following instance
I
for
\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}
, where we define (assuming
D_{A}={\left\lfloor 2^{y^{*}_{A}}\right\rfloor}
,
D_{B}={\left\lfloor 2^{y^{*}_{B}}\right\rfloor}
,
D_{C}={\left\lfloor 2^{y^{*}_{C}}\right\rfloor},M={\left\lfloor 2^{m^{*}}\right\rfloor}
)

\displaystyle R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}

\displaystyle=\{[(i-1)\cdot D_{A}+1,i\cdot D_{A}]\times[(i-1)\cdot D_{B}+1,i\cdot D_{B}]:i\in[M]\}

\displaystyle R_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}

\displaystyle=\{[(i-1)\cdot D_{B}+1,i\cdot D_{B}]\times[(i-1)\cdot D_{C}+1,i\cdot D_{C}]:i\in[M]\}

\displaystyle R_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}

\displaystyle=\{[(i-1)\cdot D_{C}+1,i\cdot D_{C}]\times[(i-1)\cdot D_{A}+1,i\cdot D_{A}]:i\in[M]\}.

Based on above, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}\right\rVert_{p}

\displaystyle=\sqrt[p]{D_{A}\cdot D_{B}^{p}\cdot M}

(140)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{y^{*}_{A}}{p}+y^{*}_{B}+\frac{m^{*}}{p}}

(141)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\log(L)}=L,

(142)

where the first inequality follows from the definition of
D_{A},D_{B}
and
M
and the second inequality follows from dual constraint above (in
\mathrm{LP}^{(**)}
). Using symmetry, we can do a similar argument for

\left\lVert R_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}\right\rVert_{p}
and
\left\lVert R_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}\right\rVert_{p}
as well.

Our instance is valid and we get a lower bound of

\displaystyle|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}|

\displaystyle\geq D_{A}\cdot D_{B}\cdot D_{C}\cdot M

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8}2^{y^{*}_{A}+y^{*}_{B}+y^{*}_{C}+m^{*}}

from it.

We now attempt to see if we can obtain an upper bound based on
\mathrm{LP}_{p}^{(**)}
as well. To do this, we come up with an upper bound
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathbf{d},G)
(for a fixed degree configuration
\mathbf{d}=(d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})})_{d_{({v}\rightarrow{u})}\leq L}
) as the minimum of the following three expressions:

\displaystyle\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}^{p}}\min\left\{d_{({A}\rightarrow{B})},\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}^{p}}\right\}\min\left\{d_{({B}\rightarrow{C})},\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}^{p}}\right\}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}^{p}}\min\left\{d_{({B}\rightarrow{C})},\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}^{p}}\right\}\min\left\{d_{({C}\rightarrow{A})},\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}^{p}}\right\}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}^{p}}\min\left\{d_{({C}\rightarrow{A})},\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({A}\rightarrow{B})}^{p}}\right\}\min\left\{d_{({A}\rightarrow{B})},\frac{2^{p}\cdot L^{p}}{d_{({B}\rightarrow{C})}^{p}}\right\}.

One natural option is to use
\mathrm{LP}^{(*)}
, which we used to upper bound
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathbf{d},G)
for the case when
p\leq|V|-1
.

\displaystyle\max z_{C}

(
\mathrm{LP}^{(*)}
)

\displaystyle z_{C}\leq z_{({A}\rightarrow{B},{p})}+y^{\prime}_{B}+y^{\prime}_{C}

\displaystyle z_{C}\leq z_{({B}\rightarrow{C},{p})}+y^{\prime}_{A}+y^{\prime}_{A}

\displaystyle z_{C}\leq z_{({C}\rightarrow{A},{p})}+y^{\prime}_{A}+y^{\prime}_{B}

\displaystyle\frac{z_{({A}\rightarrow{B},{p})}}{p}+y^{\prime}_{B}\leq\log(L)

\displaystyle\frac{z_{({B}\rightarrow{C},{p})}}{p}+y^{\prime}_{C}\leq\log(L)

\displaystyle\frac{z_{({C}\rightarrow{A},{p})}}{p}+y^{\prime}_{A}\leq\log(L)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{A}\leq z_{({A}\rightarrow{B})},y^{\prime}_{B}\leq z_{({B}\rightarrow{C})},y^{\prime}_{C}\leq z_{({C}\rightarrow{A})}

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{A},y^{\prime}_{B},y^{\prime}_{C},z^{\prime}_{A},z^{\prime}_{B},z^{\prime}_{C}\geq 0.

To argue that we can upper bound
|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}|
by
\mathrm{LP}_{p}^{(**)}
as well, we first need to prove the below result.

Lemma I.6
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathbf{d},G)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\mathrm{LP}^{(*)}}.

This result can be proved with appropriate definition of a feasible solution
(z_{({A}\rightarrow{B})},z_{({B}\rightarrow{C})},z_{({C}\rightarrow{A})})
and
(y^{\prime}_{A},y^{\prime}_{B},y^{\prime}_{C})
based on
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\mathbf{d},G)
. For the next step, we state the below conjecture (which we believe to be true):

thm I.1
.

\displaystyle 2^{\mathrm{LP}^{(*)}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\mathrm{LP}_{p}^{(**)}}

Note that if it is indeed true, by strong duality, we would get tight bounds on
|\mathrm{J_{G}^{(I)}}|
up to a polylogarithmic factor of
\log(L)^{3}
(i.e., number of degree configurations).
